But he couldn't string the bow, for his hands were soft and unused to hard work, so they quickly grew tired. He said to the suitors, "My friends, I cannot string it. Let another try. This bow will take the life and soul from many a chief among us, for it's better to die than to live after missing the prize we've striven for so long and which has kept us together so long. Some of us are even now hoping and praying to marry Penelope, but when he has seen this bow and tried it, let him court and offer wedding gifts to some other woman, and let Penelope marry whoever makes her the best offer and whose fate it is to win her."

A suitor, son of Oenops, who acts as sacrificial priest to the others.

## modern-en · eurynomus-suitor · 2:1

Aegyptius, a man bent double with age and vast experience, was the first to speak. His son Antiphus had gone with Odysseus to Troy, land of noble horses, but the savage Cyclops had killed him when they were all trapped in the cave, and had made him his last meal. He had three sons left, two of whom still worked their father's land, while the third, Eurynomus, was one of the suitors. Nevertheless their father could not get over losing Antiphus, and was still weeping for him when he began his speech.

A suitor, one of the three surviving sons of the old Ithacan Aegyptius.

## modern-en · amphimedon · 22:25

Meanwhile Agelaus son of Damastor, Eurynomus, Amphimedon, Demoptolemus, Pisander, and Polybus son of Polyctor led the fight on the suitors' side. Of all those still fighting for their lives, they were by far the most valiant, for the rest had already fallen under Odysseus's arrows. Agelaus shouted to them: "Friends, he'll have to stop soon, for Mentor has gone away after doing nothing but boast. They're standing at the doors unsupported. Don't all aim at him at once—let six of you throw your spears first, and see if you can't cover yourselves with glory by killing him. Once he falls, we needn't worry about the others."

A suitor of Ithaca, son of Melaneus, who once hosted Agamemnon.

## modern-en · polybus-eurymachus · 1:29

Then Eurymachus, son of Polybus, answered, "It's up to heaven to decide who will be king among us, but you should be master of your own house and possessions. As long as there's a man left in Ithaca, no one will use force against you or rob you. Now, my good fellow, I want to know about this stranger. What country does he come from? What family is he from, and where are his lands? Did he bring you news about your father's return, or was he here on his own business? He seemed like a well-to-do man, but he left so suddenly—gone in a moment before we could get to know him."

Father of the suitor Eurymachus.

## modern-en · leocritus · 2:14

Leocritus, son of Evenor, answered him saying, "Mentor, what folly is all this, that you should urge the people to stop us? It is a hard thing for one man to fight with many over his food. Even if Odysseus himself were to attack us while we are feasting in his house, and do his best to throw us out, his wife, who wants him back so badly, would have little cause for rejoicing, and his blood would be on his own head if he fought against such odds. There is no sense in what you have been saying. Now, therefore, you people go about your business, and let his father's old friends, Mentor and Halitherses, help this boy on his journey, if he goes at all—which I do not think he will, for he is more likely to stay where he is until someone comes and tells him something."

A suitor, son of Evenor, who breaks up the Ithacan assembly. Butler’s original spells him Leiocritus at his first appearance.

## modern-en · irus · 18:0

Now there came a common vagrant who wandered all over the city of Ithaca, begging for food, notorious as an incorrigible glutton and drunkard. This man had no strength or substance in him, but he was a big, hulking fellow to look at. His real name, the one his mother gave him, was Arnaeus, but the young men called him Irus because he would run errands for anyone who would send him. As soon as he arrived, he began insulting Odysseus and trying to drive him from his own house.

The town beggar of Ithaca, a big idle man whose real name is Arnaeus; the young men call him Irus because he runs their errands.

## modern-en · mentes · 1:7

With that, she strapped on her gleaming golden sandals that carry her swift as the wind over land and sea. She grasped her mighty bronze-tipped spear, heavy and strong, with which she conquers ranks of warriors who displease her. Then she shot down from the heights of Olympus and appeared immediately in Ithaca, at the gateway of Odysseus's house. She disguised herself as a visitor—Mentes, chief of the Taphians—and held a bronze spear in her hand. There she found the arrogant suitors sitting on the hides of oxen they had slaughtered and eaten, playing board games in front of the house. Servants and attendants bustled about waiting on them—some mixing wine with water in the bowls, others wiping down tables with wet sponges and setting them again, still others carving great quantities of meat.

Chief of the Taphians and an old guest-friend of Odysseus’s house, son of Anchialus; Athena comes to Ithaca in his shape.

## modern-en · antiphus-elder · 17:6

Telemachus went through and out of the colonnade, spear in hand—not alone, for his two swift dogs went with him. Athena blessed him with such divine presence that everyone marveled at him as he passed, and the suitors gathered around him with fair words on their lips but malice in their hearts. But he avoided them and went to sit with Mentor, Antiphus, and Halitherses, old friends of his father's house, and they made him tell them everything that had happened. An elder of Ithaca, one of the old friends of Odysseus’s house with whom Telemachus sits in the assembly.

## modern-en · pisenor-herald · 2:3

Telemachus took this speech as a good omen and rose at once, for he was bursting with what he had to say. He stood in the middle of the assembly and the good herald Pisenor brought him his staff. Then, turning to Aegyptius, "Sir," he said, "it is I, as you will shortly learn, who have called you together, for it is I who am most wronged. I have not heard of any army approaching that I would warn you about, nor is there any matter of public importance I would discuss. My grievance is purely personal, and concerns two great misfortunes that have fallen upon my house. The first is the loss of my excellent father, who was chief among all you here present, and was like a father to every one of you. The second is much more serious, and will soon utterly ruin my estate. The sons of all the chief men among you are harassing my mother to marry them against her will. They are afraid to go to her father Icarius, asking him to choose the one he likes best, and to provide marriage gifts for his daughter. Instead, day by day they keep loitering around my father's house, slaughtering our cattle, sheep, and fat goats for their feasts, and never giving a thought to how much wine they drink. No estate can withstand such recklessness. We have no Odysseus now to protect our home from harm, and I cannot hold my own against them. I will never be as good a man as he was, but I would defend myself if I had the power to do so, for I cannot endure such treatment any longer. My house is being disgraced and ruined. Have respect, therefore, for your own consciences and for public opinion. Fear too the wrath of heaven, lest the gods should be displeased and turn upon you. I beg you by Zeus and Themis, who presides over councils, do not hold back, my friends, and leave me to face this alone—unless it be that my brave father Odysseus did some wrong to the Greeks which you would now avenge on me, by helping these suitors. Moreover, if I am to be eaten out of house and home at all, I would rather you did the eating yourselves, for then I could take action against you and demand payment from house to house until I was repaid in full, whereas now I have no remedy."

The herald of the Ithacan assembly, who brings Telemachus his staff.

## modern-en · damastor · 20:28

Everyone fell silent until finally Agelaus, son of Damastor, said, "No one should take offense at what was just said or argue with it—it's quite reasonable. Stop mistreating the stranger or any other servants in the house. I'd like to say something friendly to Telemachus and his mother, which I hope both will accept: 'As long as you had reason to hope Odysseus would return someday, no one could blame you for waiting and allowing the suitors in your house. It would have been better if he had returned, but it's now clear enough that he never will. So discuss this quietly with your mother and tell her to marry the best man who makes her the most advantageous offer. Then you'll be able to manage your inheritance and eat and drink in peace, while your mother looks after some other man's house, not yours.'"

Father of the suitor Agelaus.

## modern-en · oenops · 21:10

The rest agreed, and Leiodes, son of Oenops, was the first to rise. He was the suitors' priest and sat in the corner near the wine bowl. He was the only man who hated their evil deeds and was disgusted with the others. He was now first to take the bow and arrow, so he went to the threshold to make his attempt. But he couldn't string the bow, for his hands were soft and unused to hard work, so they quickly grew tired. He said to the suitors, "My friends, I cannot string it. Let another try. This bow will take the life and soul from many a chief among us, for it's better to die than to live after missing the prize we've striven for so long and which has kept us together so long. Some of us are even now hoping and praying to marry Penelope, but when he has seen this bow and tried it, let him court and offer wedding gifts to some other woman, and let Penelope marry whoever makes her the best offer and whose fate it is to win her."

Father of the suitor Leiodes.

So they talked, and presently Hermes came up to them with the spirits of the suitors who had been killed by Odysseus. An Ithacan, father of the suitor Amphimedon.

## modern-en · nisus · 16:35

They all remained silent until Amphinomus rose to speak. He was the son of Nisus, who was King Aretias's son, and he was the leading suitor from the wheat-growing, well-pastured island of Dulichium. His conversation pleased Penelope more than that of any other suitor, for he was a man of good character. "Friends," he said, speaking plainly and honestly, "I'm not in favor of killing Telemachus. It's a terrible thing to kill someone of noble blood. Let's first consult the gods, and if Zeus's oracles advise it, I'll help kill him myself and urge everyone else to do so. But if they discourage us, I say we should hold back."

A rich and brave man of Dulichium, son of Aretias and father of Amphinomus.

## modern-en · polytherses · 22:28

The suitors threw a second volley, but again Athena made most of their weapons ineffective. One hit a pillar of the hall, another struck the door, and another's bronze point hit the wall. Still, Amphimedon managed to graze the top of Telemachus's wrist, and Ctesippus grazed Eumaeus's shoulder above his shield, though the spear passed over and fell to the ground. Then Odysseus and his men let fly into the crowd of suitors. Odysseus hit Eurydamas, Telemachus hit Amphimedon, and Eumaeus hit Polybus. After this the cowherd struck Ctesippus in the chest and taunted him: "Foul-mouthed son of Polytherses, don't be so foolish as to speak wickedly again, but let heaven guide your words, for the gods are far stronger than men. I give you this advice in return for the hoof you gave Odysseus when he was begging in his own house."

Father of the suitor Ctesippus.

## modern-en · mastor · 24:30

## modern-en · terpes · 22:33

The bard Phemius son of Terpes—who had been forced by the suitors to sing for them—now tried to save his life. He was standing near the small door, holding his lyre. He couldn't decide whether to flee from the hall and sit by Zeus's altar in the outer court, where both Laertes and Odysseus had offered the thigh bones of many oxen, or to go straight to Odysseus and clasp his knees. In the end he decided it was best to embrace Odysseus's knees. So he set his lyre on the ground between the mixing bowl and the silver-studded chair, then went up to Odysseus, grasped his knees, and said, "Odysseus, I beg you to have mercy and spare me. You'll regret it later if you kill a bard who can sing for both gods and men as I can. I compose all my songs myself, and heaven fills me with every kind of inspiration. I would sing to you as if you were a god—so don't be so eager to cut off my head. Your own son Telemachus will tell you that I didn't want to frequent your house and sing to the suitors after their meals, but they were too many and too strong for me, so they forced me."

Father of the bard Phemius.

## modern-en · ops · 1:31

The suitors then returned to their singing and dancing until evening came. But when darkness fell on their revelry, they went home to bed, each to his own dwelling. Telemachus's bedroom was high up in a tower overlooking the courtyard. He went there, brooding and deep in thought. A loyal old woman, Eurycleia, daughter of Ops the son of Pisenor, went before him carrying two blazing torches. Laertes had bought her with his own money when she was quite young, paying the price of twenty oxen, and he honored her in his household as much as his own wife, though he never shared her bed for fear of his wife's anger. She was the one who now lit Telemachus's way to his room, and she loved him more than any of the other household women, for she had nursed him as a baby. He opened his bedroom door and sat on his bed. As he took off his soft tunic, he gave it to the good old woman, who folded it neatly and hung it on a peg beside his bed. Then she left the room, closed the door by its silver handle, and slid the bolt home with its leather strap. But Telemachus lay under his woolen blanket, thinking all night long about his planned journey and the advice Athena had given him.

Father of the nurse Euryclea, son of Pisenor.

## modern-en · arceisius · 4:65

Then the dear old nurse Euryclea said, "You may kill me, my lady, or let me live on in your house, whichever you choose, but I'll tell you the truth. I knew all about it and gave him everything he needed—bread and wine—but he made me swear a solemn oath that I wouldn't tell you anything for ten or twelve days, unless you asked or happened to hear that he had gone. He didn't want you to ruin your beauty by crying. Now, my lady, wash your face, change your dress, and go upstairs with your maids to offer prayers to Athena, daughter of Zeus who carries the aegis, for she can save him even if he's in the jaws of death. Don't trouble Laertes—he has enough trouble already. Besides, I can't believe that the gods hate the family of Arceisius's son so much. There will be a descendant left to inherit both the house and the beautiful fields that stretch far around it."

Odysseus’s grandfather, father of Laertes.

Odysseus’s maternal grandfather, famous as the most accomplished thief and perjurer alive; he named the boy and gave him the boar-hunt scar.

The swineherd led the way with his master following, looking like some broken-down old tramp leaning on his staff, his clothes all in rags. When they had crossed the rough, steep ground and were approaching the city, they reached the fountain where the citizens drew their water. This had been built by Ithacus, Neritus, and Polyctor. A grove of water-loving poplars grew in a circle around it, and clear cold water flowed down to it from a high rock. Above the fountain was an altar to the nymphs where all travelers made sacrifices. Here Melanthius, son of Dolius, overtook them as he drove down some goats—the best in his flock—for the suitors' dinner, with two shepherds accompanying him. When he saw Eumaeus and Odysseus, he insulted them with outrageous and offensive language that made Odysseus very angry.

One of the three men said to have built the town fountain of Ithaca.

There's a high headland there stretching into the sea from a place called Gortyn, and all along this coast as far as Phaestus the sea runs high when there's a south wind blowing, but beyond Phaestus the coast is more protected, for a small headland can provide great shelter. ## modern-en · themis · 2:3

Telemachus took this speech as a good omen and rose at once, for he was bursting with what he had to say. He stood in the middle of the assembly and the good herald Pisenor brought him his staff. Then, turning to Aegyptius, "Sir," he said, "it is I, as you will shortly learn, who have called you together, for it is I who am most wronged. I have not heard of any army approaching that I would warn you about, nor is there any matter of public importance I would discuss. My grievance is purely personal, and concerns two great misfortunes that have fallen upon my house. The first is the loss of my excellent father, who was chief among all you here present, and was like a father to every one of you. The second is much more serious, and will soon utterly ruin my estate. The sons of all the chief men among you are harassing my mother to marry them against her will. They are afraid to go to her father Icarius, asking him to choose the one he likes best, and to provide marriage gifts for his daughter. Instead, day by day they keep loitering around my father's house, slaughtering our cattle, sheep, and fat goats for their feasts, and never giving a thought to how much wine they drink. No estate can withstand such recklessness. We have no Odysseus now to protect our home from harm, and I cannot hold my own against them. I will never be as good a man as he was, but I would defend myself if I had the power to do so, for I cannot endure such treatment any longer. My house is being disgraced and ruined. Have respect, therefore, for your own consciences and for public opinion. Fear too the wrath of heaven, lest the gods should be displeased and turn upon you. I beg you by Zeus and Themis, who presides over councils, do not hold back, my friends, and leave me to face this alone—unless it be that my brave father Odysseus did some wrong to the Greeks which you would now avenge on me, by helping these suitors. Moreover, if I am to be eaten out of house and home at all, I would rather you did the eating yourselves, for then I could take action against you and demand payment from house to house until I was repaid in full, whereas now I have no remedy."

Goddess invoked as the beginning and end of councils.

He looked black as night with his bare bow in his hands and an arrow on the string, glaring around as if always about to shoot. Around his chest was a wondrous golden belt decorated most marvelously with bears, wild boars, and lions with gleaming eyes. There was also war, battle, and death depicted on it. The craftsman who made that belt could never, no matter what he tried, make another like it. Heracles recognized me immediately and spoke sorrowfully: 'Poor Odysseus, noble son of Laertes, are you too living the same miserable kind of life I lived when I was above ground? He once sent me here to fetch the hell-hound, thinking he couldn't find anything harder for me than this. But I brought the hound out of Hades and delivered him, for Hermes and Athena helped me.'

Daughter of Zeus and Hera, married to Heracles among the gods.

## modern-en · eileithyia · 19:14

Then Odysseus answered, "Madam, wife of Odysseus, since you persist in asking me about my family, I will answer, no matter what it costs me: people must expect to be pained when they have been exiles as long as I have, and suffered as much among as many peoples. Nevertheless, as regards your question I will tell you all you ask. There is a fair and fruitful island in mid-ocean called Crete; it is thickly peopled and there are ninety cities in it: the people speak many different languages which overlap one another, for there are Achaeans, brave Eteocretans, Dorians of three-fold race, and noble Pelasgians. There is a great town there, Knossos, where Minos reigned who every nine years had a conference with Zeus himself. Minos was father to Deucalion, whose son I am, for Deucalion had two sons Idomeneus and myself. Idomeneus sailed for Troy, and I, who am the younger, am called Aethon; my brother, however, was at once the older and the more valiant of the two; hence it was in Crete that I saw Odysseus and showed him hospitality, for the winds took him there as he was on his way to Troy, carrying him out of his course from cape Malea and leaving him in Amnisus off the cave of Eileithyia, where the harbors are difficult to enter and he could hardly find shelter from the winds that were then raging. As soon as he got there he went into the town and asked for Idomeneus, claiming to be his old and valued friend, but Idomeneus had already set sail for Troy some ten or twelve days earlier, so I took him to my own house and showed him every kind of hospitality, for I had abundance of everything. Moreover, I fed the men who were with him with barley meal from the public store, and got subscriptions of wine and oxen for them to sacrifice to their heart's content. They stayed with me twelve days, for there was a gale blowing from the North so strong that one could hardly keep one's feet on land. I suppose some unfriendly god had raised it for them, but on the thirteenth day the wind dropped, and they got away."

Goddess of childbirth, who has a cave at Amnisus in Crete.

Then Zeus's daughter Helen thought of something else. She drugged the wine with a herb that banishes all care, sorrow, and bad feelings. Whoever drinks wine mixed with this drug cannot shed a single tear for the rest of the day, not even if both his father and mother died, or if he saw a brother or son cut down before his very eyes. This drug of such amazing power had been given to Helen by Polydamna, wife of Thon, a woman of Egypt, where all sorts of herbs grow—some good to put in the wine bowl and others poisonous. Moreover, everyone in that whole country is a skilled doctor, for they are descendants of Paeon. When Helen had put this drug in the bowl and told the servants to pour the wine, she said:

The healer of the gods; the Egyptian physicians are called his race.

God of wine, who gave the golden urn that holds Achilles’ bones and who denounced Ariadne.

## modern-en · atlas · 1:4

Then Athena said, "Father, son of Cronos, king of kings, Aegisthus got what he deserved, and so would anyone else who acts as he did. But Aegisthus doesn't matter to me—it's Odysseus who breaks my heart when I think of his suffering on that lonely island surrounded by sea, far from all his friends. It's a forested island in the middle of the ocean, and a goddess lives there—the daughter of Atlas the magician, who watches over the ocean depths and holds up the great pillars that separate heaven and earth. This daughter of Atlas has trapped poor unhappy Odysseus and keeps trying with every kind of charm to make him forget his home. He's grown weary of life and thinks of nothing but seeing the smoke rising from his own chimneys once more. Yet you pay no attention to this. Didn't Odysseus honor you with many burnt offerings when he was at Troy? Why do you remain so angry with him?"

Calypso’s father, who knows the depths of the sea and holds the pillars of heaven apart.

## modern-en · thoosa · 1:5

Zeus replied, "My child, what are you saying? How could I forget Odysseus? There's no mortal more capable than he, nor more generous in his offerings to the immortal gods who live in heaven. But remember that Poseidon is still furious with Odysseus for blinding the eye of Polyphemus, king of the Cyclopes. Polyphemus is Poseidon's son by the nymph Thoosa, daughter of the sea-king Phorcys. So while Poseidon won't kill Odysseus outright, he torments him by preventing him from reaching home. Still, let's put our heads together and figure out how to help him return. Then Poseidon will be appeased—if we're all in agreement, he can hardly stand against us alone."

A sea nymph, daughter of Phorcys and mother of Polyphemus by Poseidon.

Scylla’s mother, whom Odysseus is told to call on as he passes.

## modern-en · halosydne · 4:33

"'Stranger,' she said, 'I'll make it all perfectly clear. As soon as he surfaces, he lies down and sleeps in a great sea cave, where the seals—Halosydne's children, as they're called—also emerge from the gray sea and sleep in groups all around him. And they bring with them a very strong, fishy smell. Early tomorrow morning I'll take you to this place and position you in ambush. A sea goddess; the seals are called her flock.

## modern-en · perse · 10:11

From there we sailed on sadly, glad to have escaped death though we had lost our comrades, and came to the island of Aeaea, where Circe lives—a great and cunning goddess, sister to the sorcerer Aeetes. Both are children of the Sun by Perse, who is Ocean's daughter. We brought our ship into a safe harbor without a word, guided there by some god, and after landing we lay there for two days and nights, exhausted in body and spirit. When the morning of the third day came, I took my spear and sword and left the ship to scout around, hoping to find signs of human activity or hear voices. Climbing to the top of a high lookout, I spotted smoke rising from Circe's house through the dense forest. When I saw this, I wondered whether I should go investigate immediately, but in the end I decided it was better to return to the ship, give the men their meal, and send some of them instead of going myself.

Daughter of Oceanus and mother, by the sun, of Circe and Aeetes.

## modern-en · neaera · 12:10

"Next you'll reach the island of Thrinacia, where you'll see many herds of cattle and flocks of sheep belonging to the sun god—seven herds of cattle and seven flocks of sheep, fifty animals in each group. They never breed or decrease in number, and they're tended by the goddesses Phaethusa and Lampetia, daughters of the sun god Helios and the nymph Neaera. After bearing and nursing them, their mother sent them to distant Thrinacia to live there and watch over their father's flocks and herds. If you leave these animals unharmed and focus only on reaching home, you may still reach Ithaca after great hardship. But if you harm them, I predict the destruction of both your ship and your companions. Even if you yourself escape, you'll return home late, in miserable condition, having lost all your men."

A nymph, mother by Helios of Phaethusa and Lampetie.

## modern-en · maia · 14:23

With that he began chopping firewood while the others brought in a fine fat five-year-old boar and set it at the altar. Eumaeus did not forget the gods, for he was a man of good principles, so the first thing he did was cut bristles from the pig's face and throw them into the fire, praying to all the gods as he did so that Odysseus might return home again. Then he clubbed the pig with a piece of oak he had kept back when he was chopping the firewood and stunned it, while the others slaughtered and singed it. Then they cut it up, and Eumaeus began by putting raw pieces from each joint onto some of the fat. These he sprinkled with barley meal and laid upon the embers. They cut the rest of the meat up small, put the pieces on spits, and roasted them until they were done. When they had taken them off the spits, they threw them onto the serving table in a heap. The swineherd, who was a most fair-minded man, then stood up to give everyone his share. He made seven portions—one of these he set apart for Hermes the son of Maia and the nymphs, praying to them as he did so, and the others he distributed to the men one by one. He gave Odysseus some slices cut lengthwise down the loin as a mark of special honor, and Odysseus was much pleased. "I hope, Eumaeus," said he, "that Zeus will be as well disposed toward you as I am, for the respect you are showing to an outcast like myself."

Hermes’s mother.

## modern-en · erinyes · 2:7

It will be hard on me if I have to pay Icarius the large sum I would owe him if I insisted on sending his daughter back to him. Not only will he deal harshly with me, but heaven will also punish me, for my mother when she leaves the house will call on the Furies to avenge her. Besides, it would not be an honorable thing to do, and I will have nothing to do with it. If you choose to take offense at this, leave the house and feast elsewhere at one another's homes at your own expense, taking turns. If, on the other hand, you decide to persist in living off one man, heaven help me, but Zeus will settle accounts with you in full, and when you fall in my father's house there will be no one to avenge you."

The avenging spirits called down by a wronged parent. Butler’s original names them the Erinyes.

## modern-en · naiads · 13:9

When the bright star that heralds dawn's approach began to show, the ship drew near to land. Now there is a harbor in Ithaca belonging to the old sea-god Phorcys, lying between two headlands that jut into the sea and shelter the harbor. These protect it from the storms and rough seas that rage outside, so that once inside, a ship can lie safely without even needing to be moored. At the head of this harbor stands a large olive tree, and nearby is a beautiful arching cave sacred to the nymphs called Naiads. Inside are stone mixing bowls and wine jars, and bees make their hives there. There are also great stone looms where the nymphs weave their sea-purple robes—a wondrous sight to see—and fresh water always flows within. The cave has two entrances: one facing north, by which mortals can enter, while the other faces south and is more mysterious—mortals cannot possibly enter by it, as it's the path used by the gods.

The water nymphs of the cave above the harbour of Phorcys in Ithaca.

## modern-en · nestor · 1:18

"But enough! It's up to heaven to decide whether he'll return and take revenge in his own house. However, I urge you to start planning how to get rid of these suitors immediately. Take my advice: call the Greek leaders to assembly tomorrow morning, present your case to them, and call on the gods to witness your words. Tell the suitors to go away, each to his own home. If your mother is determined to remarry, let her return to her father's house—he'll find her a husband and provide all the wedding gifts such a dear daughter deserves. As for yourself, let me persuade you to take the best ship you can get with a crew of twenty men and go searching for your long-missing father. Someone might tell you something, or you might hear some heaven-sent message—that's often how people learn things. First go to Pylos and question Nestor, then continue to Sparta to visit Menelaus, who returned home last of all the Greeks. If you hear that your father is alive and coming home, you can endure these suitors' waste for another year. But if you learn of his death, return home immediately, hold proper funeral rites, build a memorial mound, and see that your mother marries again. Then, having done all this, consider carefully how you might kill these suitors in your own house, whether by cunning or open force. You're too old to plead youth as an excuse any longer. Haven't you heard how people praise Orestes for killing Aegisthus, his father's murderer? You're a fine, handsome young man—show your courage and make a name for yourself. Now I must return to my ship and crew, who will be impatient if I keep them waiting longer. Think over what I've told you, and remember my words."

The old king of Pylos, knight of Gerene, who came home safely from Troy and still gives the best counsel of any Greek.

## modern-en · pisistratus · 3:4

She then walked quickly ahead, and Telemachus followed her steps until they reached the place where the groups of Pylian people were assembled. There they found Nestor sitting with his sons, while his companions busily prepared dinner, putting pieces of meat on spits while other pieces cooked. When they saw the strangers, they crowded around them, took them by the hand, and invited them to sit down. Nestor's son Pisistratus immediately offered his hand to each of them and seated them on soft sheepskins that lay on the sand near his father and his brother Thrasymedes. Then he gave them portions of the sacred meat and poured wine into a golden cup, handing it first to Athena and greeting her respectfully.

Nestor’s youngest son, who travels with Telemachus to Sparta.

## modern-en · perseus-nestorid · 3:31

When rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, Nestor left his bed and took his seat on the benches of white polished marble that stood in front of his house. Here in former times sat Neleus, peer of gods in counsel, but he was now dead and had gone to Hades's house, so Nestor sat in his place with scepter in hand as guardian of the public good. His sons gathered around him as they left their rooms—Echephron, Stratius, Perseus, Aretus, and Thrasymedes. The sixth son was Pisistratus, and when Telemachus joined them, they seated him with them. Nestor then addressed them.

A son of Nestor.

## modern-en · eurydice · 3:34

When they'd finished praying and sprinkling barley meal, Thrasymedes struck his blow and brought the heifer down with a stroke that cut through the tendons at the base of her neck, at which Nestor's daughters and daughters-in-law, and his venerable wife Eurydice—eldest daughter of Clymenus—cried out with joy. Then they lifted the heifer's head from the ground, and Pisistratus cut her throat. When she'd finished bleeding and was quite dead, they butchered her. They cut out the thigh bones in proper order, wrapped them in two layers of fat, and placed some raw meat on top. Then Nestor laid them on the wood fire and poured wine over them, while the young men stood near him with five-pronged spits in their hands. When the thighs were burned and they'd tasted the inner organs, they cut up the rest of the meat small, put the pieces on spits, and roasted them over the fire.

Nestor’s wife, eldest daughter of Clymenus.

## modern-en · neleus · 3:0

Nestor’s father, the old king of Pylos, son of Poseidon and Tyro.

## modern-en · helen · 4:0

They reached the low-lying city of Lacedaemon, where they drove straight to the palace of Menelaus and found him in his own house, celebrating with his many relatives in honor of his son's wedding, and also his daughter's, whom he was giving in marriage to the son of that brave warrior Achilles. He had promised her to him while still at Troy, and now the gods were bringing the marriage to pass, so he was sending her with chariots and horses to the city of the Myrmidons, where Achilles' son ruled. For his only son he had found a bride from Sparta, the daughter of Alector. This son, Megapenthes, was born to him by a slave woman, for heaven granted Helen no more children after she bore Hermione, who was as beautiful as golden Aphrodite herself.

Menelaus’s wife, daughter of Zeus, whose going to Troy began the war.

## modern-en · adraste · 4:10

Adraste brought her a chair, Alcippe a soft woolen rug, while Phylo fetched her the silver work-box that Alcandra, wife of Polybus, had given her. Polybus lived in Egyptian Thebes, the richest city in the whole world. He gave Menelaus two silver baths, two tripods, and ten talents of gold. Besides all this, his wife gave Helen beautiful gifts: a golden spindle and a silver work-box on wheels with a gold rim around the top. Then Helen took her seat, put her feet on the footstool, and began questioning her husband.

Phylo now placed this beside her, filled with fine spun yarn, and a spindle loaded with violet-colored wool was laid on top of it. Then Helen took her seat, put her feet on the footstool, and began questioning her husband.

One of Helen’s maids at Sparta, who fetches her work-box.

## modern-en · diocles · 3:36

Thus he spoke, and they did exactly as he said, yoking the swift horses to the chariot. The housekeeper packed them provisions of bread, wine, and delicacies fit for princes. Then Telemachus got into the chariot while Pisistratus gathered up the reins and took the seat beside him. He whipped the horses on, and they flew forward eagerly into the open country, leaving the high citadel of Pylos behind them. All day they traveled, with the yoke swaying on their necks, until the sun set and darkness covered the land. Then they reached Pherae, where Diocles lived—son of Ortilochus and grandson of Alpheus. Here they spent the night, and Diocles entertained them hospitably. When rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, they again harnessed their horses and drove out through the gateway under the echoing portico. Pisistratus whipped the horses on, and they flew forward eagerly. Soon they came to the wheat lands of the open country, and in due time completed their journey, so well did their steeds carry them.

The host at Pherae on the road between Pylos and Sparta, son of Ortilochus.

## modern-en · alector · 4:0

They reached the low-lying city of Lacedaemon, where they drove straight to the palace of Menelaus and found him in his own house, celebrating with his many relatives in honor of his son's wedding, and also his daughter's, whom he was giving in marriage to the son of that brave warrior Achilles. He had promised her to him while still at Troy, and now the gods were bringing the marriage to pass, so he was sending her with chariots and horses to the city of the Myrmidons, where Achilles' son ruled. For his only son he had found a bride from Sparta, the daughter of Alector. A Spartan, father of the bride Megapenthes marries.

## modern-en · phrontis · 3:23

"Meanwhile Menelaus and I were on our way home from Troy, on good terms with each other. When we reached Sunium, which is the point of Attica, Apollo with his painless arrows killed Phrontis, Menelaus's helmsman—no man knew better how to handle a vessel in rough weather—so he died right there with the helm in his hand. Menelaus, though very anxious to press on, had to wait to bury his comrade and give him proper funeral rites. When he could put to sea again and had sailed as far as the Malean headlands, Zeus plotted evil against him and made it blow hard until the waves ran mountain-high. Here he divided his fleet, taking half toward Crete where the Cydonians live around the waters of the river Iardanus. There's a high headland there stretching into the sea from a place called Gortyn, and all along this coast as far as Phaestus the sea runs high when there's a south wind blowing, but beyond Phaestus the coast is more protected, for a small headland can provide great shelter. Here this part of the fleet was driven onto the rocks and wrecked, but the crews just managed to save themselves. As for the other five ships, they were carried by winds and seas to Egypt, where Menelaus gathered much gold and treasure among people of foreign speech. Meanwhile Aegisthus here at home plotted his evil deed. For seven years after he killed Agamemnon, he ruled in Mycenae with the people obedient to him, but in the eighth year Orestes returned from Athens to be his doom, and killed his father's murderer. Then he held a funeral feast for his mother and false Aegisthus for the people of Argos, and on that very day Menelaus came home with as much treasure as his ships could carry.

The steersman of Menelaus’s ship, the best helmsman alive, killed by Apollo off Sunium.

## modern-en · alcinous · 6:0

So here Odysseus slept, overcome by exhaustion and weariness, but Athena traveled to the country and city of the Phaeacians—a people who used to live in the beautiful town of Hypereia, near the lawless Cyclopes. The Cyclopes were stronger than they were and constantly raided them, so their king Nausithous moved them away and settled them in Scheria, far from all other peoples. He built a wall around the city, constructed houses and temples, and distributed the land among his people. But he had died and gone to the realm of the dead, and King Alcinous, whose wisdom was blessed by heaven, now ruled. Athena went to his house to further Odysseus' return home.

King of the Phaeacians on Scheria, son of Nausithous and husband of Arete.

## modern-en · arete · 7:5

"This is the house, dear stranger, that you asked me to show you. You'll find many great people dining inside, but don't be afraid—go straight in, for the bolder a man is, the more likely he is to succeed, even if he's a stranger. First find the queen. Her name is Arete, and she comes from the same family as her husband Alcinous. They both descend from Poseidon, who fathered Nausithous by Periboea, a woman of great beauty. Periboea was the youngest daughter of Eurymedon, who once ruled over the giants, but he destroyed his ill-fated people and lost his own life as well.

Queen of the Phaeacians, Alcinous’s wife and niece, honoured by her people beyond any other woman.

## modern-en · nausicaa · 6:1

Athena took the form of the famous sea captain Dymas's daughter, who was Nausicaa's close friend and the same age. Then, approaching the girl's bedside like a gentle breeze, she hovered over her head and spoke:

Alcinous and Arete’s daughter, who finds Odysseus on the shore.

## modern-en · demodocus · 8:2

"Listen to me, leaders and councilors of the Phaeacians, so I may speak my mind. This stranger, whoever he may be, has come to my house from somewhere east or west. He needs an escort and wants the matter settled. Let us prepare one for him, as we have done for others before. Indeed, no one who has ever come to my house has been able to complain that I was slow in speeding them on their way. Let us launch a ship—one that has never made a voyage—and crew it with fifty-two of our smartest young sailors. When you have secured your oars to your benches, leave the ship and come to my house for a feast. I will provide everything. These are my instructions to the young men who will form the crew. As for you leaders and councilors, you will join me in entertaining our guest in the courtyard. I will accept no excuses, and we will have Demodocus sing for us, for there is no bard like him, whatever he chooses to sing about."

The blind bard of the Phaeacian court, whom the Muse loves.

## modern-en · laodamas · 7:15

When Alcinous heard this, he took Odysseus by the hand, raised him from the hearth, and had him take the seat of Laodamas, who had been sitting beside him and was his favorite son. A maidservant brought him water in a beautiful golden pitcher and poured it into a silver basin for him to wash his hands, and she set a polished table beside him. An upper servant brought bread and offered him many good things from what was in the house, and Odysseus ate and drank. Then Alcinous said to one of the servants, "Pontonous, mix a cup of wine and pass it around so we can make drink offerings to Zeus the Thunderer, who protects all worthy suppliants."

Alcinous’s son and his favourite, the best looking of the young Phaeacians.

A servant hung Demodocus's lyre on its peg, led him out of the courtyard, and set him on the same path that all the Phaeacian leaders were taking to watch the sports. A crowd of several thousand people followed them, and there were many excellent competitors for all the events. There was Acroneus, Ocyalus, Elatreus, Nauteus, Prymneus, Anchialus, Eretmeus, Ponteus, Proreus, Thoon, Anabesineus, and Amphialus son of Polyneus son of Tecton. There was also Euryalus son of Naubolus, who was like Ares himself and was the handsomest man among the Phaeacians except Laodamas. Three sons of Alcinous also competed: Laodamas, Halios, and Clytoneus.

A young Phaeacian who competes in the games held for Odysseus.

## modern-en · polyphemus · 1:5

Zeus replied, "My child, what are you saying? How could I forget Odysseus? There's no mortal more capable than he, nor more generous in his offerings to the immortal gods who live in heaven. But remember that Poseidon is still furious with Odysseus for blinding the eye of Polyphemus, king of the Cyclopes. Polyphemus is Poseidon's son by the nymph Thoosa, daughter of the sea-king Phorcys. So while Poseidon won't kill Odysseus outright, he torments him by preventing him from reaching home. Still, let's put our heads together and figure out how to help him return. Then Poseidon will be appeased—if we're all in agreement, he can hardly stand against us alone."

The Cyclops whose cave Odysseus enters, son of Poseidon and the nymph Thoosa.

## modern-en · telemus · 9:38

"At this he groaned, and cried out, 'Alas, alas, then the old prophecy about me is coming true. There was a prophet here, at one time, a man both brave and of great stature, Telemus son of Eurymus, who was an excellent seer, and did all the prophesying for the Cyclopes until he grew old; he told me that all this would happen to me some day, and said I should lose my sight by the hand of Odysseus. I have been all along expecting someone of imposing presence and superhuman strength, whereas he turns out to be a little insignificant weakling, who has managed to blind my eye by taking advantage of me in my drink; come here, then, Odysseus, that I may make you presents to show my hospitality, and urge Poseidon to help you forward on your journey—for Poseidon and I are father and son. He, if he so chooses, shall heal me, which no one else neither god nor man can do.'

A seer among the Cyclopes, son of Eurymus, who once prophesied to Polyphemus.

## modern-en · eurymus · 9:38

## modern-en · noman · 9:23

"I then gave him some more; three times did I fill the bowl for him, and three times did he drain it without thought or care; then, when I saw that the wine had gotten into his head, I said to him as persuasively as I could: 'Cyclops, you ask my name and I will tell it to you; give me, therefore, the present you promised me; my name is Nobody; this is what my father and mother and my friends have always called me.'

The name Odysseus gives the Cyclops when asked. The modern edition renders it Nobody.

## modern-en · aeolus · 10:0

From there we sailed to the floating island of Aeolus, son of Hippotas, beloved by the immortal gods. It's an island that seems to drift on the sea, surrounded by an unbreakable wall of bronze. Aeolus had six daughters and six strong sons, and he married the sons to the daughters, so they all lived together with their parents in constant celebration, enjoying every luxury imaginable. All day long the house filled with the rich smell of roasting meat, and the courtyard echoed with the sounds of feasting. At night they slept in their fine beds, each man with his wife beside him. These were the people we had come among.

Son of Hippotas and keeper of the winds on his floating island; he gives Odysseus the bag of storms.

## modern-en · hippotas · 10:0

From there we sailed to the floating island of Aeolus, son of Hippotas, beloved by the immortal gods. It's an island that seems to drift on the sea, surrounded by an unbreakable wall of bronze. Aeolus had six daughters and six strong sons, and he married the sons to the daughters, so they all lived together with their parents in constant celebration, enjoying every luxury imaginable. ## modern-en · antiphates · 10:9

When my men reached the shore, they followed a smooth road that the people used to bring firewood down from the mountains to the town. Soon they met a young woman who had come out to fetch water—she was the daughter of a Laestrygonian named Antiphates. She was going to the spring called Artacia, where the people got their water, and when my men approached her, they asked who ruled that land and what kind of people lived there. She directed them to her father's house, but when they got there, they found his wife was a giantess as huge as a mountain, and they were horrified at the sight of her.

King of the Laestrygonians, who begins the destruction of Odysseus’s fleet.

## modern-en · eurylochus · 10:14

Their hearts sank when they heard me, remembering how they had been treated by the Laestrygonian Antiphates and by the savage giant Polyphemus. They wept bitterly in their despair, but crying wouldn't help, so I divided them into two groups and appointed a leader for each. I put Eurylochus in charge of one group while I commanded the other. Then we drew lots in a helmet, and the lot fell to Eurylochus. So he set out with his twenty-two men, and they wept, as did we who were left behind.

Odysseus’s second in command and kinsman by marriage; he argues against his captain twice.

## modern-en · polites · 10:15

When they reached Circe's house, they found it built of polished stones in a clearing that could be seen from far off, in the middle of the forest. Wild mountain wolves and lions prowled all around it—poor enchanted creatures that she had tamed with her magic and drugged into submission. They didn't attack my men, but wagged their great tails, fawned on them, and rubbed against them lovingly. Just as dogs crowd around their master when they see him coming back from dinner—knowing he'll bring them scraps—so these wolves and lions with their great claws fawned on my men, though the men were terrified at seeing such strange beasts. Soon they reached the gates of the goddess's house, and standing there they could hear Circe inside, singing beautifully as she worked at her loom, weaving cloth so fine, so soft, and of such brilliant colors that only a goddess could make. Then Polites, whom I valued and trusted more than any of my other men, said, "Someone inside is working at a loom and singing beautifully—the whole place echoes with it. Let's call out and see whether it's a woman or goddess."

The companion Odysseus valued most of all, who speaks first at Circe’s door.

## modern-en · perimedes · 11:1

Here Perimedes and Eurylochus held the sacrificial animals while I drew my sword and dug a trench a cubit square. I made drink offerings to all the dead—first with honey and milk, then with wine, and finally with water. I sprinkled white barley meal over everything, praying earnestly to the helpless ghosts and promising them that when I returned to Ithaca, I would sacrifice my best barren heifer for them and load the funeral pyre with fine offerings. I especially promised Teiresias the finest black sheep from my flocks. When I had finished praying to the dead, I cut the throats of the two sheep and let their blood flow into the trench. Then the ghosts came streaming up from the underworld—young brides, unmarried men, old men worn out by toil, maidens who had died for love, and brave warriors killed in battle with their armor still stained with blood. They came from every direction and flitted around the trench with a strange, unearthly wailing that made me pale with fear. When I saw them approaching, I ordered my men to quickly skin the dead sheep and burn them as offerings while reciting prayers to Hades and Persephone. But I sat with my drawn sword, refusing to let the helpless ghosts near the blood until Teiresias had answered my questions.

One of Odysseus’s crew, who helps at the trench of the dead and at the Sirens.

## modern-en · elpenor · 10:46

Even so, I didn't get them all away without accident. We had with us a young man named Elpenor, not particularly notable for good sense or courage, who had gotten drunk and was lying on the rooftop away from the other men, sleeping off his wine in the cool air. When he heard the noise of the men bustling about, he jumped up suddenly and forgot to come down by the main staircase, so he fell straight off the roof and broke his neck, and his soul went down to the house of Hades.

The youngest of Odysseus’s crew, neither brave nor clever, who falls from Circe’s roof and is the first ghost to meet him.

## modern-en · teiresias · 10:40

The goddess answered, "Noble Odysseus, son of Laertes, none of you need stay here any longer against your will, but there's another journey you must make before you can sail home. You must go to the house of Hades and dread Persephone to consult the spirit of the blind Theban prophet Tiresias, whose mind is still sound. To him alone has Persephone left his understanding even in death, while the other ghosts flit about mindlessly."

The blind Theban prophet, the one dead man whose understanding Persephone left whole; Odysseus goes to Hades to consult him. The modern edition also spells the name Tiresias.

## modern-en · scylla · 12:6

"Of the two rocks, one reaches to heaven, its peak lost in dark clouds that never leave it, so the summit is never clear, not even in summer and early autumn. No man, even with twenty hands and twenty feet, could find a foothold and climb it, for it rises straight up, smooth as if polished. Halfway up is a large cave facing west toward the underworld. You must steer your ship past this way, but the cave is so high that even the strongest archer couldn't shoot an arrow into it. Inside, Scylla sits howling with a voice like a young puppy's, but she is actually a dreadful monster that no one—not even a god—could face without terror. She has twelve misshapen feet and six necks of enormous length. At the end of each neck is a hideous head with three rows of closely packed teeth that could crush anyone to death instantly. She sits deep in her shadowy cave, thrusting out her heads and scanning the rock all around, fishing for dolphins, sharks, or any larger sea creature she can catch from the thousands that swarm in these waters. No ship has ever passed her without losing men, for she shoots out all her heads at once and carries off a man in each mouth.

The six-headed monster in the cliff above the strait, daughter of Crataiis.

## modern-en · charybdis · 12:7

"The other rock lies lower, but they're so close together there's barely a bowshot between them. A large fig tree grows on it in full leaf, and beneath it lies Charybdis' sucking whirlpool. Three times each day she spits up the waters, and three times she sucks them down again. Make sure you're not there when she's sucking them down, for if you are, not even Poseidon could save you. You must hug Scylla's side and drive your ship past as fast as possible, because it's better to lose six men than your entire crew."

The whirlpool under the fig tree opposite Scylla, which sucks the water down three times a day.

## modern-en · sirens · 12:4

"So far, so good," she said when I had finished my story. "Now pay attention to what I'm about to tell you—heaven itself will help you remember it. First, you'll come to the Sirens, who enchant everyone who approaches them. Anyone who carelessly draws too close and hears the Sirens singing will never see his wife and children welcome him home again, for the Sirens sit in a flowery meadow and bewitch him to death with the sweetness of their song. Around them lies a great heap of men's bones with flesh still rotting on them. So sail past these Sirens, and stop your men's ears with wax so none of them can hear. But if you want to listen yourself, have your men bind you upright to the mast's crossbeam, and they must tie the rope ends to the mast itself so you can enjoy listening. If you beg and plead with them to untie you, they must bind you even tighter.

The singers on their meadow who enchant every man who hears them.

## modern-en · muse · 1:0

Tell me, Muse, of that clever hero who wandered far and wide after he destroyed the famous city of Troy. He visited many cities and learned the customs of many peoples. He suffered greatly at sea while trying to save his own life and bring his men home safely. But no matter what he did, he could not save his companions—they died through their own reckless folly when they ate the cattle of the sun god Helios. So the god prevented them from ever reaching home. Tell me about all these things, daughter of Zeus, from whatever source you know them.

The goddess of song, invoked in the first line and named as the source of Demodocus’s gift.

## modern-en · maron · 9:11

"I told my men to draw the ship ashore, and stay where they were, all but the twelve best among them, who were to go along with me. I also took a goatskin of sweet black wine which had been given me by Maron, son of Euanthes, who was priest of Apollo the patron god of Ismarus, and lived within the sacred precincts of the temple. When we were sacking the city we respected him, and spared his life, as well as his wife and child; so he made me some presents of great value—seven talents of fine gold, and a bowl of silver, with twelve jars of sweet wine, unblended, and of the most exquisite flavor. Not a man or woman in the house knew about it, but only himself, his wife, and one housekeeper: when he drank it he mixed twenty parts of water to one of wine, and yet the fragrance from the mixing-bowl was so wonderful that it was impossible to resist drinking. I filled a large skin with this wine, and took a bag full of provisions with me, for my mind warned me that I might have to deal with some savage who would be of great strength, and would respect neither right nor law.

Priest of Apollo at Ismarus, son of Euanthes, who gave Odysseus the strong wine out of gratitude.

## modern-en · aeetes · 10:11

From there we sailed on sadly, glad to have escaped death though we had lost our comrades, and came to the island of Aeaea, where Circe lives—a great and cunning goddess, sister to the sorcerer Aeetes. Both are children of the Sun by Perse, who is Ocean's daughter. We brought our ship into a safe harbor without a word, guided there by some god, and after landing we lay there for two days and nights, exhausted in body and spirit. When the morning of the third day came, I took my spear and sword and left the ship to scout around, hoping to find signs of human activity or hear voices. Climbing to the top of a high lookout, I spotted smoke rising from Circe's house through the dense forest. When I saw this, I wondered whether I should go investigate immediately, but in the end I decided it was better to return to the ship, give the men their meal, and send some of them instead of going myself.

A magician, Circe’s brother, and child like her of the sun and Perse.

## modern-en · jason · 12:5

"After your crew takes you past the Sirens, I cannot give you clear directions about which of two routes to take. I'll present both alternatives, and you must decide for yourself. On one side are towering rocks against which the deep blue waves crash with terrible fury—the blessed gods call these the Wandering Rocks. Not even birds can pass here, not even the gentle doves that bring ambrosia to Father Zeus. The sheer rock always destroys one of them, and Father Zeus must send another to replace it. No ship that has ever reached these rocks has escaped. The waves and fiery whirlwinds carry away wreckage and men's bodies. Only one vessel ever sailed through safely—the famous Argo returning from Aeetes' land, and she would have been dashed against those mighty rocks if Hera hadn't guided her past them for love of Jason.

The hero for whose sake Hera steered the Argo safely past the wandering rocks.

## modern-en · lamus · 10:7

From there we sailed on miserably until the men were worn out from long and useless rowing, since there was no longer any wind to help them. For six days and nights we struggled, and on the seventh day we reached the rocky fortress of Lamus—Telepylus, city of the Laestrygonians, where the shepherd driving his flocks home for milking greets the one driving his animals out to pasture, and the other returns the greeting. In that country, a man who never slept could earn double wages—one as a cattle herder and another as a shepherd, since they work almost the same hours by night as by day.

The founder whose rocky stronghold is the Laestrygonian city.

## modern-en · agamemnon · 1:2

Now Poseidon had gone to the Ethiopians, who live at the world's edge and are divided in two groups, one facing west and the other east. He had gone there to receive an offering of a hundred sheep and oxen, and was enjoying himself at the feast. But the other gods gathered in the palace of Olympian Zeus, and the father of gods and men spoke first. At that moment he was thinking of Aegisthus, who had been killed by Agamemnon's son Orestes. So he said to the other gods:

King of Mycenae and leader of the Greeks at Troy, Menelaus’s brother; he came home to an ambush.

## modern-en · clytemnestra · 3:21

"I'll tell you truly," Nestor answered, "and you've already guessed how it all happened. If Menelaus had found Aegisthus still alive in his house when he returned from Troy, there would have been no burial mound heaped up for him—not even when he was dead. He would have been thrown outside the city for dogs and vultures, and no woman would have mourned him, for he had committed a terrible crime. But we were over there fighting hard at Troy, and Aegisthus, taking his ease quietly in the heart of Argos, seduced Agamemnon's wife Clytemnestra with constant flattery.

Agamemnon’s wife, daughter of Tyndareus, who took Aegisthus while her husband was at Troy.

## modern-en · aegisthus · 1:2

He had gone there to receive an offering of a hundred sheep and oxen, and was enjoying himself at the feast. But the other gods gathered in the palace of Olympian Zeus, and the father of gods and men spoke first. At that moment he was thinking of Aegisthus, who had been killed by Agamemnon's son Orestes. The man who seduced Clytemnestra and killed Agamemnon, and was killed in turn by Orestes.

## modern-en · orestes · 1:2

But the other gods gathered in the palace of Olympian Zeus, and the father of gods and men spoke first. At that moment he was thinking of Aegisthus, who had been killed by Agamemnon's son Orestes. So he said to the other gods:

Agamemnon’s son, who avenged his father; the example held up to Telemachus.

## modern-en · achilles · 3:11

"My friend," Nestor answered, "you bring back memories of great sorrow, for the brave Greeks suffered much both at sea while raiding under Achilles, and fighting before the great city of King Priam. All our best men fell there—Ajax, Achilles, Patroclus who was like a god in counsel, and my own dear son Antilochus, remarkably swift of foot and brave in battle. But we suffered much more than this. What mortal tongue could tell the whole story? Even if you stayed here questioning me for five or six years, I couldn't tell you all that the Greeks endured, and you'd turn homeward weary of my tale before it ended. For nine long years we tried every kind of strategy, but heaven's hand was against us. During all this time, no one could match your father in cleverness—if you are indeed his son—I can hardly believe my eyes—and you speak just like him too. No one would think people of such different ages could speak so similarly. He and I never disagreed from first to last, neither in camp nor council, but with one heart and purpose we advised the Greeks how everything might turn out for the best.

Son of Peleus and the greatest of the Greek fighters at Troy.

## modern-en · peleus · 11:39

As we sat weeping and talking sadly together, the ghost of Achilles approached with Patroclus, Antilochus, and Ajax, who was the finest and most handsome of all the Greeks after Peleus's son. The fleet descendant of Aeacus recognized me and spoke mournfully: 'Odysseus, noble son of Laertes, what daring deed will you attempt next, that you venture down to Hades among us mindless dead, who are merely ghosts of those who can no longer work?'

Achilles’ father, still ruling among the Myrmidons.

## modern-en · aeacus · 11:39

As we sat weeping and talking sadly together, the ghost of Achilles approached with Patroclus, Antilochus, and Ajax, who was the finest and most handsome of all the Greeks after Peleus's son. The fleet descendant of Aeacus recognized me and spoke mournfully: 'Odysseus, noble son of Laertes, what daring deed will you attempt next, that you venture down to Hades among us mindless dead, who are merely ghosts of those who can no longer work?'

Peleus’s father; Achilles is called his descendant.

Even if you stayed here questioning me for five or six years, I couldn't tell you all that the Greeks endured, and you'd turn homeward weary of my tale before it ended. For nine long years we tried every kind of strategy, but heaven's hand was against us. During all this time, no one could match your father in cleverness—if you are indeed his son—I can hardly believe my eyes—and you speak just like him too. No one would think people of such different ages could speak so similarly. He and I never disagreed from first to last, neither in camp nor council, but with one heart and purpose we advised the Greeks how everything might turn out for the best.

Son of Telamon and the finest of the Greeks after Achilles; he lost the contest for Achilles’ armour and will not speak to Odysseus even in death.

As for what happened on the battlefield—you were there yourself. A third Greek leader is still alive but stranded at sea, unable to return. Ajax was shipwrecked when Poseidon drove him onto the great rocks of Gyrae. Even so, Poseidon let him get safely out of the water, and despite all Athena's hatred, he would have escaped death if he hadn't ruined himself by boasting. He said the gods couldn't drown him even though they tried, and when Poseidon heard this arrogant talk, he grabbed his trident in both powerful hands and split the rock of Gyrae in two. The base remained where it was, but the part Ajax was sitting on fell headlong into the sea, carrying Ajax with it. So he swallowed salt water and drowned.

The Greek leader wrecked on the rocks of Gyrae on the voyage home.

"Therefore, my dear young friend, I returned without hearing anything about the others. They say the Myrmidons returned home safely under Achilles's son Neoptolemus; so did brave Philoctetes, son of Poias. Idomeneus lost no men at sea, and all his followers who escaped death in battle got safely home with him to Crete. No matter how remote you live, you'll have heard of Agamemnon and the terrible end he met at Aegisthus's hands—and what a fearful reckoning Aegisthus paid for it. See what a good thing it is for a man to leave a son behind him to do as Orestes did, who killed false Aegisthus, his noble father's murderer. You too—for you're a tall, handsome fellow—show your courage and make yourself famous in story."

Achilles’ son, whom Odysseus fetched from Scyros; he came home from Troy unharmed.

## modern-en · diomedes · 3:14

"That night we rested and nursed our anger, for Zeus was plotting trouble for us. But in the morning, some of us launched our ships and put our goods and women aboard, while the rest—about half—stayed behind with Agamemnon. We, the other half, embarked and sailed, and the ships went well, for heaven had calmed the sea. When we reached Tenedos we offered sacrifices to the gods, longing to get home. But cruel Zeus didn't yet mean for us to do so, and raised a second quarrel during which some among us turned their ships back and sailed away under Odysseus to make peace with Agamemnon. But I and all the ships with me pressed forward, for I saw that trouble was brewing. Tydeus's son also went on with me, and his crews with him. Later Menelaus joined us at Lesbos and found us deciding our course—for we didn't know whether to go outside Chios by the island of Psyra, keeping it to our left, or inside Chios, past the stormy headland of Mimas. So we asked heaven for a sign and were shown that we'd be soonest out of danger if we headed our ships across the open sea to Euboea. This we did, and a fair wind sprang up that gave us a quick passage during the night to Geraestus, where we offered many sacrifices to Poseidon for helping us so far. Four days later Diomedes and his men stationed their ships in Argos, but I held on for Pylos, and the wind never failed from the day heaven first made it favorable for me.

Son of Tydeus, who stationed his ships at Argos on the way home. Butler’s original spells him Diomed.

## modern-en · cassandra · 11:35

'Odysseus, noble son of Laertes,' he answered, 'I wasn't lost at sea in any storm Poseidon raised, nor did enemies kill me on the mainland. Aegisthus and my wicked wife murdered me together. He invited me to his house, feasted me, then butchered me as miserably as a fat beast in a slaughterhouse, while all around me my companions were slain like sheep or pigs for a wedding feast, banquet, or celebration of some great lord. You must have seen many men killed in battle or single combat, but you never saw anything as truly pitiful as how we fell in that hall, with the wine bowl and loaded tables lying about and the ground reeking with our blood. I heard Priam's daughter Cassandra scream as Clytemnestra killed her beside me. I lay dying on the ground with a sword in my body and raised my hands to strike the murderous slut, but she slipped away from me. She wouldn't even close my lips or eyes as I died, for nothing in this world is as cruel and shameless as a woman who has fallen into such guilt as hers. Imagine murdering her own husband! I thought I'd be welcomed home by my children and servants, but her abominable crime has brought disgrace on herself and all women who come after—even the good ones.'

Priam’s daughter, killed beside Agamemnon by Clytemnestra.

## modern-en · deiphobus · 4:21

Then Menelaus said, "Everything you've said, my dear wife, is true. I've traveled extensively and dealt with many heroes, but I've never seen another man like Odysseus. What endurance and courage he showed inside the wooden horse, where all the bravest Greeks were hiding, waiting to bring death and destruction to the Trojans! At that moment you came to us—some god who favored the Trojans must have prompted you, and Deiphobus was with you. Three times you walked around our hiding place and patted it, calling each of our leaders by name and imitating all our wives' voices. Diomedes, Odysseus, and I heard what a racket you made from inside. Diomedes and I couldn't decide whether to jump out right then or answer you from inside, but Odysseus kept us all in check, so we sat perfectly still. Only Anticlus started to answer you, but Odysseus clamped his strong hands over his mouth and kept them there. That's what saved us all, for he silenced Anticlus until Athena led you away."

A Trojan who was with Helen when she called to the men inside the wooden horse.

## modern-en · eurypylus · 11:42

'I've heard nothing about Peleus,' I answered, 'but I can tell you all about your son Neoptolemus, for I brought him in my own ship from Scyros to join the Greeks. In our war councils before Troy, he was always first to speak, and his judgment was flawless. Only Nestor and I could surpass him. When it came to fighting on Troy's plain, he would never stay with the main body of troops but would dash far ahead, foremost of all in courage. He killed many men in battle—I can't name every single one he slew while fighting for the Greeks, but I'll mention how he killed that brave hero Eurypylus, son of Telephus, the handsomest man I ever saw except Memnon. Many other Ceteans fell around Eurypylus because of a woman's bribes. Moreover, when all the bravest Greeks went inside the horse Epeus had built, and it fell to me to decide when we should open our ambush or stay hidden, though all the other leaders and chief men among the Greeks were wiping their eyes and trembling in every limb, I never once saw him turn pale or brush a tear from his cheek. He kept urging me to break out from the horse, gripping his sword handle and bronze-tipped spear, breathing fury against the enemy. Yet when we had sacked Priam's city, he received his fair share of the prize money and boarded his ship (such is war's fortune) without a wound, neither from a thrown spear nor in close combat, for the rage of war is very unpredictable.'

Son of Telephus and the handsomest man Odysseus ever saw except Memnon; killed by Neoptolemus at Troy.

## modern-en · eurybates · 19:16

"Madam," answered Odysseus, "it is such a long time ago that I can hardly say. Twenty years are come and gone since he left my home, and went elsewhither; but I will tell you as well as I can recollect. Odysseus wore a mantle of purple wool, double lined, and it was fastened by a gold brooch with two catches for the pin. On the face of this there was a device that showed a dog holding a spotted fawn between his fore paws, and watching it as it lay panting upon the ground. Every one marveled at the way in which these things had been done in gold, the dog looking at the fawn, and strangling it, while the fawn was struggling convulsively to escape. As for the shirt that he wore next his skin, it was so soft that it fitted him like the skin of an onion, and glistened in the sunlight to the admiration of all the women who beheld it. Furthermore I say, and lay my saying to your heart, that I do not know whether Odysseus wore these clothes when he left home, or whether one of his companions had given them to him while he was on his voyage; or possibly someone at whose house he was staying made him a present of them, for he was a man of many friends and had few equals among the Achaeans. I myself gave him a sword of bronze and a beautiful purple mantle, double lined, with a shirt that went down to his feet, and I sent him on board his ship with every mark of honor. He had a servant with him, a little older than himself, and I can tell you what he was like; his shoulders were hunched, he was dark, and he had thick curly hair. His name was Eurybates, and Odysseus treated him with greater familiarity than he did any of the others, as being the most like-minded with himself."

Odysseus’s herald at Troy, round-shouldered, dark and curly-haired; his master valued him above the rest.

"This was what she said, and we agreed. So we could see her working on her great web all day long, but at night she would unpick the stitches again by torchlight. She fooled us this way for three years without our knowing, but as time passed and she was in her fourth year, one of her maids who knew what she was doing told us, and we caught her in the act of undoing her work, so she had to finish it whether she wanted to or not. Therefore, the suitors give you this answer, so that both you and all the Greeks may understand: 'Send your mother away, and tell her to marry the man of her own and her father's choice.' For I do not know what will happen if she goes on tormenting us much longer with the airs she gives herself because of the skills Athena has taught her, and because she is so clever. We never heard of such a woman before. We know all about Tyro, Alcmene, Mycene, and the famous women of old, but not one of them could match your mother. It was not fair of her to treat us this way, and as long as she continues in the mind which heaven has given her, we will go on consuming your estate. And I do not see why she should change, for she gets all the honor and glory, and it is you who pay for it, not she. Understand, then, that we will not go back to our lands, neither here nor elsewhere, until she has made her choice and married one of us."

Daughter of Salmoneus and wife of Cretheus, who bore Pelias and Neleus to Poseidon in the likeness of the river Enipeus.

## modern-en · salmoneus · 11:17

The first I saw was Tyro, daughter of Salmoneus and wife of Cretheus, son of Aeolus. She fell in love with the river Enipeus, the most beautiful river in the whole world. Once when she was walking by its banks as usual, Poseidon, disguised as her beloved river, made love to her at the river's mouth, and a huge blue wave arched itself like a mountain over them to hide both woman and god. Then he loosened her virgin belt and made her sleep deeply. When the god had completed his act of love, he took her hand and said, "Tyro, rejoice in good will—the embraces of gods are not fruitless, and around this time next year you'll have fine twin sons. Take great care of them. I am Poseidon, so now go home, but keep silent and don't tell anyone."

Tyro’s father.

## modern-en · itylus · 19:33

"Stranger, I should like to speak with you briefly about another matter. It is indeed nearly bed time—for those, at least, who can sleep in spite of sorrow. As for myself, heaven has given me a life of such unmeasurable woe, that even by day when I am attending to my duties and looking after the servants, I am still weeping and lamenting during the whole time; then, when night comes, and we all of us go to bed, I lie awake thinking, and my heart becomes a prey to the most incessant and cruel tortures. As the dun nightingale, daughter of Pandareus, sings in the early spring from her seat in shadiest covert hid, and with many a plaintive trill pours out the tale how by mishap she killed her own child Itylus, son of king Zethus, even so does my mind toss and turn in its uncertainty whether I ought to stay with my son here, and safeguard my substance, my bondsmen, and the greatness of my house, out of regard to public opinion and the memory of my late husband, or whether it is not now time for me to go with the best of these suitors who are wooing me and making me such magnificent presents. As long as my son was still young, and unable to understand, he would not hear of my leaving my husband's house, but now that he is full grown he begs and prays me to do so, being incensed at the way in which the suitors are eating up his property. Listen, then, to a dream that I have had and interpret it for me if you can. I have twenty geese about the house that eat mash out of a trough, and of which I am exceedingly fond. I dreamed that a great eagle came swooping down from a mountain, and dug his curved beak into the neck of each of them till he had killed them all. Presently he soared off into the sky, and left them lying dead about the yard; whereon I wept in my dream till all my maids gathered round me, so piteously was I grieving because the eagle had killed my geese. Then he came back again, and perching on a projecting rafter spoke to me with human voice, and told me to leave off crying. 'Be of good courage,' he said, 'daughter of Icarius; this is no dream, but a vision of good omen that shall surely come to pass. The geese are the suitors, and I am no longer an eagle, but your own husband, who am come back to you, and who will bring these suitors to a disgraceful end.' On this I woke, and when I looked out I saw my geese at the trough eating their mash as usual."

Zethus’s son, killed by mishap by his own mother, who became the nightingale.
