## modern-en · alcmena · 2:6

"This was what she said, and we agreed. So we could see her working on her great web all day long, but at night she would unpick the stitches again by torchlight. She fooled us this way for three years without our knowing, but as time passed and she was in her fourth year, one of her maids who knew what she was doing told us, and we caught her in the act of undoing her work, so she had to finish it whether she wanted to or not. Therefore, the suitors give you this answer, so that both you and all the Greeks may understand: 'Send your mother away, and tell her to marry the man of her own and her father's choice.' For I do not know what will happen if she goes on tormenting us much longer with the airs she gives herself because of the skills Athena has taught her, and because she is so clever. We never heard of such a woman before. We know all about Tyro, Alcmene, Mycene, and the famous women of old, but not one of them could match your mother. It was not fair of her to treat us this way, and as long as she continues in the mind which heaven has given her, we will go on consuming your estate. And I do not see why she should change, for she gets all the honor and glory, and it is you who pay for it, not she. Understand, then, that we will not go back to our lands, neither here nor elsewhere, until she has made her choice and married one of us."

Wife of Amphitryon and mother of Heracles by Zeus.

## modern-en · epicaste · 11:21

I also saw fair Epicaste, mother of King Oedipus, whose awful fate it was to marry her own son unknowingly. He married her after killing his father, but the gods revealed the whole story to the world. He remained king of Thebes in great grief for the gods' spite against him, but Epicaste went to mighty Hades' house, having hanged herself in her anguish, leaving behind avenging spirits to torment him for his outraged mother—bringing him bitter regret thereafter.

Mother of Oedipus, who married her own son without knowing it.

## modern-en · oedipus · 11:21

## modern-en · chloris · 11:22

Then I saw Chloris, whom Neleus married for her beauty, giving priceless gifts for her. She was the youngest daughter of Amphion, son of Iasus and king of Minyan Orchomenus, and became queen of Pylos. She bore Nestor, Chromius, and Periclymenus, and also that marvelously lovely woman Pero, who was courted by all the surrounding country. But Neleus would only give her to whoever could steal Iphicles' cattle from the grazing grounds of Phylace—a difficult task. The only man willing to attempt this cattle raid was a certain excellent seer, but heaven's will was against him. The cattle guards captured and imprisoned him. Nevertheless, when a full year had passed and the same season came again, Iphicles freed him after he had interpreted all of heaven's oracles. Thus Zeus's will was accomplished.

Neleus’s wife and queen in Pylos, youngest daughter of Amphion of Orchomenus; mother of Nestor.

## modern-en · iphimedeia · 11:24

After her I saw Iphimedeia, wife of Aloeus, who boasted of Poseidon's embrace. She bore two sons, Otus and Ephialtes, but both were short-lived. They were the finest and best-looking children ever born in this world, except for Orion. At nine years old they were nine fathoms tall and nine cubits around the chest. They threatened to make war on the Olympian gods and tried to pile Mount Ossa on top of Mount Olympus, and Mount Pelion on top of Ossa, so they could scale heaven itself. They would have succeeded if they'd reached maturity, but Apollo, son of Leto, killed them both before they had even a trace of hair on their cheeks or chins.

Wife of Aloeus, who bore Otus and Ephialtes to Poseidon.

## modern-en · peirithous · 11:53

With that, Heracles went back down into Hades' house, but I stayed where I was in case some other mighty dead might come to me. I would have seen still others who are gone—Theseus and Pirithous, glorious children of the gods—but so many thousands of ghosts gathered around me uttering such terrifying cries that I was struck with panic, fearing Persephone might send up from Hades' house the head of that awful monster, the Gorgon. At this I hurried back to my ship and ordered my men to board immediately and cast off the mooring ropes. So they embarked and took their places, and the ship went down the stream of the river Ocean. We had to row at first, but soon a fair wind sprang up.

Theseus’s companion, host to the Centaur Eurytion among the Lapiths.

## modern-en · eurytion · 21:26

This made them all very angry, for they feared he might string the bow. Antinous therefore rebuked him fiercely, saying, "Wretched creature, you don't have so much as a grain of sense in your whole body. You should think yourself lucky to be allowed to dine unharmed among your betters, without having any smaller portion served to you than we others have had, and to be allowed to hear our conversation. No other beggar or stranger has been allowed to hear what we say among ourselves. The wine must be affecting you badly, as it does with all those who drink too much. It was wine that inflamed the Centaur Eurytion when he was staying with Peirithous among the Lapiths. When the wine got into his head, he went mad and did terrible things in Peirithous's house. This angered the heroes assembled there, so they rushed at him and cut off his ears and nose, then dragged him through the doorway out of the house. So he went away crazed, bearing the burden of his crime, robbed of understanding. From then on, there was war between mankind and the centaurs, but he brought it on himself through his own drunkenness. In the same way, I can tell you it will go badly for you if you string the bow. You'll find no mercy from anyone here, for we'll immediately ship you off to King Echetus, who kills everyone who comes near him. You'll never escape alive, so drink and keep quiet without picking a fight with men younger than yourself."

The Centaur whom wine inflamed at Peirithous’s house.

## modern-en · tityus · 7:25

"Stranger," replied Alcinous, "I'm not the kind of man to get angry over nothing—it's always better to be reasonable. But by Father Zeus, Athena, and Apollo, now that I see what kind of person you are and how much you think as I do, I wish you would stay here, marry my daughter, and become my son-in-law. If you'll stay, I'll give you a house and estate. But no one—heaven forbid—will keep you here against your will, and to assure you of this, I'll arrange your escort tomorrow. You can sleep during the entire voyage if you like, and my men will sail you over calm waters either to your own home or wherever you please, even if it's much farther than Euboea, which those of my people who saw it when they took golden-haired Rhadamanthus to visit Tityus, son of Earth, tell me is the most distant place—yet they made the whole voyage in a single day without tiring themselves and returned afterward. You'll see how much my ships excel all others and what magnificent oarsmen my sailors are."

A giant son of Gaia, punished in Hades for assaulting Leto.

## modern-en · tantalus · 11:50

I also saw Tantalus's dreadful fate. He stood in a lake that reached his chin, dying of thirst but never able to reach the water. Whenever the poor wretch bent down to drink, it dried up and vanished, leaving nothing but parched ground—withered by heaven's spite. Tall trees hung their fruit over his head—pears, pomegranates, apples, sweet figs, and juicy olives—but whenever the poor creature reached out to take some, the wind tossed the branches back up to the clouds.

A shade punished in Hades with water and fruit that withdraw whenever he reaches.

## modern-en · sisyphus · 11:51

I saw Sisyphus at his endless task, pushing his enormous stone with both hands. With hands and feet he struggled to roll it up the hill, but always, just before he could push it over to the other side, its weight overcame him, and the pitiless stone would thunder back down to the plain. Then he would start trying to push it uphill again, sweat pouring off him and steam rising from his body.

A shade punished in Hades by rolling a stone that always runs back down.

## modern-en · heracles · 8:15

Odysseus was glad when he found he had a friend among the spectators, so he began to speak more pleasantly. "Young men," he said, "match that throw if you can, and I'll throw another discus just as heavy or even heavier. If anyone wants to have a contest with me, let him come forward, for I'm extremely angry. I'll box, wrestle, or run—I don't care which—with any of you except Laodamas, but not with him because I'm his guest, and one can't compete with one's own host. At least I don't think it's wise or sensible for a guest to challenge his host's family at any game, especially in a foreign country. He'll undermine his own position if he does. But I make no exception for anyone else, for I want to settle this matter and see who's the best man. I'm skilled at every kind of athletic sport known to mankind. I'm an excellent archer. In battle I'm always the first to bring down a man with my arrow, no matter how many others are aiming at him alongside me. Philoctetes was the only man who could shoot better than I when we Greeks were before Troy and practicing. I far surpass everyone else in the whole world among those who still eat bread on the face of the earth, but I wouldn't want to compete against the mighty dead, such as Heracles or Eurytus of Oechalia—men who could shoot against the gods themselves. This is actually how Eurytus met his premature end, for Apollo was angry with him and killed him because he challenged the god as an archer. I can throw a spear farther than anyone else can shoot an arrow. Running is the only area where I fear some of the Phaeacians might beat me, for I've been brought very low at sea. My provisions ran short, and so I'm still weak."

The greatest of the strong men, whose phantom Odysseus meets in Hades while he himself feasts among the gods. Butler’s original calls him Hercules.

The monstrous head Odysseus fears Persephone will send up out of Hades.

## modern-en · rhadamanthus · 4:46

"'The third man,' he answered, 'is Odysseus who lives in Ithaca. I can see him on an island, grieving bitterly in the house of the nymph Calypso, who's keeping him prisoner. He can't reach his home because he has no ships or sailors to carry him across the sea. As for your own fate, Menelaus, you won't die in Argos. Instead, the gods will take you to the Elysian Fields at the world's end. There fair-haired Rhadamanthus rules, and people live more easily than anywhere else in the world, for in Elysium there's no rain, hail, or snow. Ocean constantly sends a gentle west wind that sings softly from the sea and brings fresh life to everyone. This will happen to you because you married Helen and are Zeus's son-in-law.'

The fair-haired ruler of the Elysian plain.

## modern-en · orion · 5:9

Calypso trembled with rage at these words. "You gods should be ashamed of yourselves," she cried. "You're always jealous and hate seeing a goddess fall for a mortal man and live with him openly. When rosy-fingered Dawn loved Orion, you precious gods were furious until Artemis killed him in Ortygia. When Demeter fell in love with Iasion and lay with him in a thrice-plowed field, Zeus heard about it quickly and killed Iasion with his thunderbolts. Now you're angry with me because I have a man here. I found the poor creature sitting alone on a ship's keel after Zeus struck his ship with lightning and sunk it mid-ocean, drowning his entire crew while he was driven by wind and waves to my island. I grew fond of him and cared for him, planning to make him immortal so he'd never age. But I can't cross Zeus or frustrate his plans. So if he insists, let the man sail beyond the seas again. But I can't send him anywhere myself since I have no ships or men to take him. Still, I'll gladly give him good advice that will help him reach his own country safely."

The hunter loved by Dawn and killed by Artemis in Ortygia; he hunts still among the dead.

Dawn’s consort, from whose side she rises each morning.

## modern-en · erechtheus · 7:8

Then Athena left Scheria and went away over the sea. She traveled to Marathon and the broad streets of Athens, where she entered Erechtheus's palace. But Odysseus went on to Alcinous's house, and he thought deeply as he paused before reaching the bronze threshold, for the palace's splendor was like that of the sun or moon. The walls on both sides were bronze from end to end, with a cornice of blue enamel. The doors were gold, hanging on silver pillars that rose from a bronze floor, while the lintel was silver and the door handle was gold.

The king of Athens to whose house Athena goes.

## modern-en · lampus · 23:19

Then Odysseus melted in turn and wept as he held his dear and faithful wife. As the sight of land is welcome to swimmers making for shore when Poseidon has wrecked their ship with furious winds and waves—a few reach land alone, covered with brine, and are thankful to find themselves on solid ground and out of danger—just so welcome was her husband to her as she looked at him, and she couldn't take her arms from around his neck. Indeed, they would have gone on sharing their joy until rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, if Athena hadn't decided otherwise and held night back in the far west, while not allowing Dawn to leave Ocean or harness her two horses Lampus and Phaethon that bring daylight to mankind.

One of the two horses that draw Dawn’s chariot.

## modern-en · actor · 23:18

When she heard the clear proof Odysseus gave her, she broke down completely. She ran weeping to his side, threw her arms around his neck, and kissed him. "Don't be angry with me, Odysseus," she cried, "you who are the wisest of all mankind. We have both suffered. Heaven has denied us the happiness of spending our youth and growing old together. So don't be upset or offended that I didn't embrace you as soon as I saw you. I've been trembling with fear the whole time that someone might come here and deceive me with a lying story, for there are many wicked people around. Helen, Zeus's daughter, would never have given herself to a foreign man if she had known that the Greeks would come after her and bring her back. Heaven put it in her heart to do wrong, and she gave no thought to that sin which has been the source of all our sorrows. But now that you've convinced me by showing you know all about our bed—which no human being has ever seen except you and me and one maid, Actor's daughter, whom my father gave me when I married and who guards our bedroom door—stubborn as I've been, I can doubt no longer."

Father of the maidservant who keeps Penelope’s bedroom door.

## modern-en · melampus · 15:19

They did as he said and went aboard. But as Telemachus was busy making prayers and sacrifices to Athena in the ship's stern, there came to him a man from a distant country—a prophet fleeing from Argos because he had killed a man. He was descended from Melampus, who used to live in Pylos, the land of sheep. Melampus had been rich and owned a great house, but was driven into exile by the great and powerful king Neleus. Neleus seized his goods and held them for a whole year, during which Melampus was imprisoned in the house of King Phylacus, suffering greatly both because of Neleus's daughter and because he was haunted by a great sorrow that the dread Fury had laid upon him. In the end, however, he escaped with his life, drove the cattle from Phylace to Pylos, avenged the wrong done to him, and gave Neleus's daughter to his brother. Then he left the country and went to Argos, where he was destined to rule over many people. There he married, established himself, and had two famous sons: Antiphates and Mantius. Antiphates became father of Oecleus, and Oecleus of Amphiaraus, who was dearly loved by both Zeus and Apollo, but didn't live to old age, for he was killed in Thebes because of a woman's bribes. His sons were Alcmaeon and Amphilochus. Mantius, Melampus's other son, was father to Polypheides and Cleitus. Golden-throned Dawn carried off Cleitus for his beauty, so he might dwell among the immortals, but Apollo made Polypheides the greatest prophet in the world now that Amphiaraus was dead. He quarreled with his father and went to live in Hyperesia, where he remained and prophesied for all people.

The seer and rich man of Pylos, driven out by Neleus, from whom Theoclymenus is descended.

## modern-en · castor-hylax · 14:11

"I am by birth a Cretan. My father was a well-to-do man who had many sons born in marriage, whereas I was the son of a slave woman he had bought as a concubine. Nevertheless, my father Castor, son of Hylax—whose lineage I claim and who was held in the highest honor among the Cretans for his wealth, prosperity, and the valor of his sons—put me on the same level as my brothers who had been born in wedlock. When death took him to the house of Hades, his sons divided his estate and cast lots for their shares, but to me they gave a holding and little else. Nevertheless, my valor enabled me to marry into a rich family, for I was not given to boasting or shirking on the battlefield. It's all over now, but if you look at the straw you can see what the grain was like, for I have had trouble enough and to spare. Ares and Athena made me mighty in war. When I had picked my men to surprise the enemy with an ambush, I never gave death so much as a thought but was the first to leap forward and spear all whom I could overtake. Such was I in battle, but I didn't care about farm work or the frugal home life of those who would raise children. My delight was in ships, fighting, javelins, and arrows—things that most men shudder to think of. But one man likes one thing and another likes another, and this was what I was most naturally inclined to. Before the Greeks went to Troy, nine times I was in command of men and ships on foreign service, and I accumulated much wealth. I had my pick of the spoils first, and much more was allotted to me later on.

The rich and honoured Cretan whom Odysseus, in his false story, claims as his father.

## modern-en · aethon · 19:14

Then Odysseus answered, "Madam, wife of Odysseus, since you persist in asking me about my family, I will answer, no matter what it costs me: people must expect to be pained when they have been exiles as long as I have, and suffered as much among as many peoples. Nevertheless, as regards your question I will tell you all you ask. There is a fair and fruitful island in mid-ocean called Crete; it is thickly peopled and there are ninety cities in it: the people speak many different languages which overlap one another, for there are Achaeans, brave Eteocretans, Dorians of three-fold race, and noble Pelasgians. There is a great town there, Knossos, where Minos reigned who every nine years had a conference with Zeus himself. Minos was father to Deucalion, whose son I am, for Deucalion had two sons Idomeneus and myself. Idomeneus sailed for Troy, and I, who am the younger, am called Aethon; my brother, however, was at once the older and the more valiant of the two; hence it was in Crete that I saw Odysseus and showed him hospitality, for the winds took him there as he was on his way to Troy, carrying him out of his course from cape Malea and leaving him in Amnisus off the cave of Eileithyia, where the harbors are difficult to enter and he could hardly find shelter from the winds that were then raging. As soon as he got there he went into the town and asked for Idomeneus, claiming to be his old and valued friend, but Idomeneus had already set sail for Troy some ten or twelve days earlier, so I took him to my own house and showed him every kind of hospitality, for I had abundance of everything. Moreover, I fed the men who were with him with barley meal from the public store, and got subscriptions of wine and oxen for them to sacrifice to their heart's content. They stayed with me twelve days, for there was a gale blowing from the North so strong that one could hardly keep one's feet on land. I suppose some unfriendly god had raised it for them, but on the thirteenth day the wind dropped, and they got away."

The name Odysseus gives Penelope when he tells her he is a Cretan and Idomeneus’s younger brother.

There is a fair and fruitful island in mid-ocean called Crete; it is thickly peopled and there are ninety cities in it: the people speak many different languages which overlap one another, for there are Achaeans, brave Eteocretans, Dorians of three-fold race, and noble Pelasgians. There is a great town there, Knossos, where Minos reigned who every nine years had a conference with Zeus himself. Minos was father to Deucalion, whose son I am, for Deucalion had two sons Idomeneus and myself. Idomeneus sailed for Troy, and I, who am the younger, am called Aethon; my brother, however, was at once the older and the more valiant of the two; hence it was in Crete that I saw Odysseus and showed him hospitality, for the winds took him there as he was on his way to Troy, carrying him out of his course from cape Malea and leaving him in Amnisus off the cave of Eileithyia, where the harbors are difficult to enter and he could hardly find shelter from the winds that were then raging. As soon as he got there he went into the town and asked for Idomeneus, claiming to be his old and valued friend, but Idomeneus had already set sail for Troy some ten or twelve days earlier, so I took him to my own house and showed him every kind of hospitality, for I had abundance of everything. Moreover, I fed the men who were with him with barley meal from the public store, and got subscriptions of wine and oxen for them to sacrifice to their heart's content. They stayed with me twelve days, for there was a gale blowing from the North so strong that one could hardly keep one's feet on land. I suppose some unfriendly god had raised it for them, but on the thirteenth day the wind dropped, and they got away."

Named in Odysseus’s false story as Minos’s son and the father of Idomeneus.

My ship is lying over there, off the open country outside the town, and this is the fifth year since Odysseus left my country. Poor fellow, yet the omens were good for him when he left me. The name Odysseus gives Laertes before telling him the truth.

"The ship ran before a fresh north wind until we had reached the sea that lies between Crete and Libya. There, however, Zeus planned their destruction, for as soon as we were well out from Crete and could see nothing but sea and sky, he raised a black cloud over our ship and the sea grew dark beneath it. Then Zeus let fly with his thunderbolts and the ship spun round and round and was filled with fire and brimstone as the lightning struck it. The men all fell into the sea and were carried about in the water around the ship, looking like so many seagulls, but the god soon deprived them of all chance of getting home again. I was dismayed, but Zeus sent the ship's mast within my reach, which saved my life, for I clung to it and drifted before the fury of the gale. For nine days I drifted, but in the darkness of the tenth night a great wave bore me onto the Thesprotian coast. There Pheidon, king of the Thesprotians, entertained me hospitably without charging me anything at all—for his son found me when I was nearly dead with cold and fatigue, lifted me by the hand, took me to his father's house, and gave me clothes to wear.

King of the Thesprotians, who in Odysseus’s tale entertained him and had news of the real Odysseus.

They meant no harm, but the wind drove them off course, and we sailed until we arrived here by night. We could barely get into the harbor, and none of us mentioned supper though we badly wanted it. We all just went ashore and lay down as we were. I was exhausted and fell asleep immediately, so they took my goods from the ship and placed them beside me where I was lying on the sand. Then they sailed away to Sidonia, leaving me here in great distress."

Idomeneus’s son, whom the beggar claims in his story to have killed in Crete.

I'll box, wrestle, or run—I don't care which—with any of you except Laodamas, but not with him because I'm his guest, and one can't compete with one's own host. At least I don't think it's wise or sensible for a guest to challenge his host's family at any game, especially in a foreign country. He'll undermine his own position if he does. But I make no exception for anyone else, for I want to settle this matter and see who's the best man. I'm skilled at every kind of athletic sport known to mankind. I'm an excellent archer. In battle I'm always the first to bring down a man with my arrow, no matter how many others are aiming at him alongside me. Philoctetes was the only man who could shoot better than I when we Greeks were before Troy and practicing. I far surpass everyone else in the whole world among those who still eat bread on the face of the earth, but I wouldn't want to compete against the mighty dead, such as Heracles or Eurytus of Oechalia—men who could shoot against the gods themselves. This is actually how Eurytus met his premature end, for Apollo was angry with him and killed him because he challenged the god as an archer. I can throw a spear farther than anyone else can shoot an arrow. Running is the only area where I fear some of the Phaeacians might beat me, for I've been brought very low at sea. Menelaus overheard him and said, "No one, my boys, can compete with Zeus, for his house and everything about him is immortal. But among mortal men—well, there may be another who has as much wealth as I do, or there may not. I went to Cyprus, Phoenicia, and Egypt; I also went to the Ethiopians, the Sidonians, and the Erembians, and to Libya where the lambs grow horns as soon as they're born and the sheep give birth three times a year. Everyone in that country, whether master or servant, has plenty of cheese, meat, and good milk, for the ewes produce all year round. Whoever your parents may be, they must have told you about all this, and about my terrible loss in the destruction of a stately mansion that was fully and magnificently furnished. I wish I had only a third of what I now possess, if only I had stayed home and all those men were still alive who died on the plain of Troy, far from Argos. I often grieve as I sit here in my house for each and every one of them. Sometimes I cry aloud in sorrow, but then I stop again, for weeping brings cold comfort and one soon grows tired of it. Yet however much I grieve for all of them, I mourn for one man more than all the rest. I can't even think of him without losing my appetite for food and sleep, so wretched does he make me feel, for none of the Greeks worked harder or risked more than he did. He gained nothing by it and left me a legacy of sorrow, for he's been gone so long that we don't know whether he's alive or dead. The people of Sidon.

Then Zeus spread panic among my men, and they would no longer face the enemy, for they found themselves surrounded. The Egyptians killed many of us and took the rest alive to do forced labor for them. Zeus, however, put it in my mind to do this—and I wish I had died then and there in Egypt instead, for there was much sorrow in store for me—I took off my helmet and shield and dropped my spear from my hand, then I went straight up to the king's chariot, clasped his knees and kissed them. He spared my life, told me to get into his chariot, and took me weeping to his own home. Many came at me with their ash spears and tried to kill me in their fury, but the king protected me, for he feared the wrath of Zeus the protector of strangers, who punishes those who do evil.

The people of Egypt, skilled in medicine.

He went up to Odysseus and took his right hand: "Good day, father stranger. You seem very unfortunate now, but I hope you'll have better times ahead. Father Zeus, you're the most cruel of all gods. I broke into a sweat when I saw this man, and my eyes filled with tears, because he reminds me of Odysseus, who I fear is wandering in just such rags as these, if he's still alive. If he's already dead in Hades' house, then alas for my good master, who made me his herdsman when I was young among the Cephallenians. Now his cattle are countless—no one could have done better with them than I have, for they've multiplied like ears of grain. Yet I must keep bringing them for others to eat, men who don't care about his son though he's in the house, and don't fear heaven's wrath, but are already eager to divide Odysseus's property since he's been gone so long. I've often thought—though it wouldn't be right while his son lives—of taking the cattle to some foreign country. As bad as that would be, it's harder to stay here and be mistreated over other people's herds. The people over whom Odysseus and Laertes ruled.

He said I hadn't served his father loyally at Troy as a vassal, but had set myself up as an independent ruler. So I ambushed him with one of my followers by the roadside and speared him as he was coming into town from the countryside. As soon as I'd done it, I went to a ship and begged the owners, who were Phoenicians, to take me aboard and set me down in Pylos or Elis where the Epeans rule, offering them spoils that satisfied them. They meant no harm, but the wind drove them off course, and we sailed until we arrived here by night. We could barely get into the harbor, and none of us mentioned supper though we badly wanted it. We all just went ashore and lay down as we were. I was exhausted and fell asleep immediately, so they took my goods from the ship and placed them beside me where I was lying on the sand. Then they sailed away to Sidonia, leaving me here in great distress."

The people who rule in Elis.

## modern-en · cretans · 14:11

"I am by birth a Cretan. My father was a well-to-do man who had many sons born in marriage, whereas I was the son of a slave woman he had bought as a concubine. Nevertheless, my father Castor, son of Hylax—whose lineage I claim and who was held in the highest honor among the Cretans for his wealth, prosperity, and the valor of his sons—put me on the same level as my brothers who had been born in wedlock. When death took him to the house of Hades, his sons divided his estate and cast lots for their shares, but to me they gave a holding and little else. Nevertheless, my valor enabled me to marry into a rich family, for I was not given to boasting or shirking on the battlefield. It's all over now, but if you look at the straw you can see what the grain was like, for I have had trouble enough and to spare. Ares and Athena made me mighty in war. When I had picked my men to surprise the enemy with an ambush, I never gave death so much as a thought but was the first to leap forward and spear all whom I could overtake. Such was I in battle, but I didn't care about farm work or the frugal home life of those who would raise children. My delight was in ships, fighting, javelins, and arrows—things that most men shudder to think of. But one man likes one thing and another likes another, and this was what I was most naturally inclined to. Before the Greeks went to Troy, nine times I was in command of men and ships on foreign service, and I accumulated much wealth. I had my pick of the spoils first, and much more was allotted to me later on.

The people of Crete, among whom the beggar claims his family stood high.

It was wine that inflamed the Centaur Eurytion when he was staying with Peirithous among the Lapiths. When the wine got into his head, he went mad and did terrible things in Peirithous's house. This angered the heroes assembled there, so they rushed at him and cut off his ears and nose, then dragged him through the doorway out of the house. So he went away crazed, bearing the burden of his crime, robbed of understanding. From then on, there was war between mankind and the centaurs, but he brought it on himself through his own drunkenness. In the same way, I can tell you it will go badly for you if you string the bow. You'll find no mercy from anyone here, for we'll immediately ship you off to King Echetus, who kills everyone who comes near him. The people among whom the Centaur Eurytion grew violent with wine. The modern edition calls them the Lapiths.

## modern-en · cimmerians · 11:0

When we reached the seashore, we dragged our ship into the water and loaded her mast and sails. We put the sheep aboard and took our places, weeping with heavy hearts. Circe, that great and cunning goddess, sent us a fair wind that blew steadily behind us, keeping our sails filled. We did whatever work the ship required and let her sail as the wind and helmsman directed. All day her sails stayed full as she held course across the sea, but when the sun set and darkness covered the earth, we entered the deep waters of the river Ocean, where lies the land and city of the Cimmerians. They live wrapped in mist and darkness that the sun's rays never pierce, neither when he rises nor when he sets again in the heavens. Those wretched people live in eternal, melancholy night. The people who live wrapped in mist and darkness at the edge of Oceanus.

## modern-en · erembians · 4:8

Menelaus overheard him and said, "No one, my boys, can compete with Zeus, for his house and everything about him is immortal. But among mortal men—well, there may be another who has as much wealth as I do, or there may not. But in any case, I've traveled far and endured much hardship, for it took nearly eight years before I could get home with my fleet. I went to Cyprus, Phoenicia, and Egypt; I also went to the Ethiopians, the Sidonians, and the Erembians, and to Libya where the lambs grow horns as soon as they're born and the sheep give birth three times a year. Everyone in that country, whether master or servant, has plenty of cheese, meat, and good milk, for the ewes produce all year round. But while I was traveling and gathering great wealth among these peoples, my brother was secretly and brutally murdered through the treachery of his wicked wife, so I take no pleasure in being lord of all this wealth. Whoever your parents may be, they must have told you about all this, and about my terrible loss in the destruction of a stately mansion that was fully and magnificently furnished. I wish I had only a third of what I now possess, if only I had stayed home and all those men were still alive who died on the plain of Troy, far from Argos. I often grieve as I sit here in my house for each and every one of them. Sometimes I cry aloud in sorrow, but then I stop again, for weeping brings cold comfort and one soon grows tired of it. Yet however much I grieve for all of them, I mourn for one man more than all the rest. I can't even think of him without losing my appetite for food and sleep, so wretched does he make me feel, for none of the Greeks worked harder or risked more than he did. He gained nothing by it and left me a legacy of sorrow, for he's been gone so long that we don't know whether he's alive or dead. His old father, his long-suffering wife Penelope, and his son Telemachus, whom he left behind as an infant, are all plunged in grief on his account."

One of the peoples Menelaus visited in his wanderings.

## modern-en · messenians · 21:0

Athena now put it into Penelope's mind to make the suitors test their skill with the bow and iron axes in a contest among themselves, as a way to bring about their destruction. She went upstairs and got the storeroom key, which was made of bronze with an ivory handle. Then she went with her servants to the storeroom at the end of the house, where her husband's treasures of gold, bronze, and wrought iron were kept, and where his bow was also stored, along with the quiver full of deadly arrows that had been given to him by a friend he had met in Lacedaemon—Iphitus, son of Eurytus. The two had encountered each other in Messene at the house of Ortilochus, where Odysseus was staying to recover a debt owed by the whole people. The Messenians had stolen three hundred sheep from Ithaca and sailed away with them and their shepherds. To recover these sheep, Odysseus had taken a long journey while still quite young, sent on this mission by his father and the other chiefs. Iphitus had also gone there to try to get back twelve brood mares he had lost, along with their mule foals. These mares would be his death in the end, for when he went to the house of Zeus's son, mighty Heracles, who performed such incredible feats of valor, Heracles shamefully killed him, though he was his guest. He feared neither heaven's vengeance nor respected his own table that he had set before Iphitus, but killed him despite everything and kept the mares for himself. It was while claiming these mares that Iphitus met Odysseus and gave him the bow that mighty Eurytus had carried, which had been left to his son when he died. Odysseus gave him in return a sword and spear, and this was the beginning of a strong friendship, though they never visited each other's homes, for Zeus's son Heracles killed Iphitus before they could do so. This bow, given to him by Iphitus, had not been taken by Odysseus when he sailed for Troy. He had used it as long as he lived at home, but had left it behind as a keepsake from a valued friend.

The people who carried off three hundred Ithacan sheep.

## modern-en · eteocretans · 19:14

Then Odysseus answered, "Madam, wife of Odysseus, since you persist in asking me about my family, I will answer, no matter what it costs me: people must expect to be pained when they have been exiles as long as I have, and suffered as much among as many peoples. Nevertheless, as regards your question I will tell you all you ask. There is a fair and fruitful island in mid-ocean called Crete; it is thickly peopled and there are ninety cities in it: the people speak many different languages which overlap one another, for there are Achaeans, brave Eteocretans, Dorians of three-fold race, and noble Pelasgians. There is a great town there, Knossos, where Minos reigned who every nine years had a conference with Zeus himself. Minos was father to Deucalion, whose son I am, for Deucalion had two sons Idomeneus and myself. Idomeneus sailed for Troy, and I, who am the younger, am called Aethon; my brother, however, was at once the older and the more valiant of the two; hence it was in Crete that I saw Odysseus and showed him hospitality, for the winds took him there as he was on his way to Troy, carrying him out of his course from cape Malea and leaving him in Amnisus off the cave of Eileithyia, where the harbors are difficult to enter and he could hardly find shelter from the winds that were then raging. As soon as he got there he went into the town and asked for Idomeneus, claiming to be his old and valued friend, but Idomeneus had already set sail for Troy some ten or twelve days earlier, so I took him to my own house and showed him every kind of hospitality, for I had abundance of everything. Moreover, I fed the men who were with him with barley meal from the public store, and got subscriptions of wine and oxen for them to sacrifice to their heart's content. They stayed with me twelve days, for there was a gale blowing from the North so strong that one could hardly keep one's feet on land. I suppose some unfriendly god had raised it for them, but on the thirteenth day the wind dropped, and they got away."

One of the peoples of Crete.

## modern-en · ceteans · 11:42

'I've heard nothing about Peleus,' I answered, 'but I can tell you all about your son Neoptolemus, for I brought him in my own ship from Scyros to join the Greeks. In our war councils before Troy, he was always first to speak, and his judgment was flawless. Only Nestor and I could surpass him. When it came to fighting on Troy's plain, he would never stay with the main body of troops but would dash far ahead, foremost of all in courage. He killed many men in battle—I can't name every single one he slew while fighting for the Greeks, but I'll mention how he killed that brave hero Eurypylus, son of Telephus, the handsomest man I ever saw except Memnon. Many other Ceteans fell around Eurypylus because of a woman's bribes. Moreover, when all the bravest Greeks went inside the horse Epeus had built, and it fell to me to decide when we should open our ambush or stay hidden, though all the other leaders and chief men among the Greeks were wiping their eyes and trembling in every limb, I never once saw him turn pale or brush a tear from his cheek. He kept urging me to break out from the horse, gripping his sword handle and bronze-tipped spear, breathing fury against the enemy. Yet when we had sacked Priam's city, he received his fair share of the prize money and boarded his ship (such is war's fortune) without a wound, neither from a thrown spear nor in close combat, for the rage of war is very unpredictable.'

The people who fell around Eurypylus at Troy. The modern edition calls them the Ceteans.

## modern-en · cydonians · 3:23

"Meanwhile Menelaus and I were on our way home from Troy, on good terms with each other. When we reached Sunium, which is the point of Attica, Apollo with his painless arrows killed Phrontis, Menelaus's helmsman—no man knew better how to handle a vessel in rough weather—so he died right there with the helm in his hand. Menelaus, though very anxious to press on, had to wait to bury his comrade and give him proper funeral rites. When he could put to sea again and had sailed as far as the Malean headlands, Zeus plotted evil against him and made it blow hard until the waves ran mountain-high. Here he divided his fleet, taking half toward Crete where the Cydonians live around the waters of the river Iardanus. There's a high headland there stretching into the sea from a place called Gortyn, and all along this coast as far as Phaestus the sea runs high when there's a south wind blowing, but beyond Phaestus the coast is more protected, for a small headland can provide great shelter. Here this part of the fleet was driven onto the rocks and wrecked, but the crews just managed to save themselves. As for the other five ships, they were carried by winds and seas to Egypt, where Menelaus gathered much gold and treasure among people of foreign speech. Meanwhile Aegisthus here at home plotted his evil deed. For seven years after he killed Agamemnon, he ruled in Mycenae with the people obedient to him, but in the eighth year Orestes returned from Athens to be his doom, and killed his father's murderer. Then he held a funeral feast for his mother and false Aegisthus for the people of Argos, and on that very day Menelaus came home with as much treasure as his ships could carry.

A people of Crete, living around the river Iardanus.
